Table 5
AI 2041: My Haunting Idol — Autonomous Driving, Virtual Rescue, and Human Judgment
1. Q: What is the central dilemma explored in Chapter 5, “My Haunting Idol,” and its story of “The Holy Driver”?
A: Show answer
The chapter examines how advanced autonomous-driving systems may depend on human judgment when roads, sensors, networks, or circumstances fall outside normal operating assumptions. Chamal is recruited because his virtual racing skill appears useful for a rescue-driving programme, but the company disguises the real purpose as a game and exposes him to high-stakes simulated missions. He later discovers that the simulations are connected to real emergencies and that human operators may be asked to intervene when autonomous systems fail. The story therefore connects technological capability with questions of consent, safety, employment, responsibility, and the value of human agency. Its central tension is not simply whether AI can drive, but who bears the consequences when a system needs human help.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the technical, human, economic, and ethical questions raised by Chamal’s recruitment and final rescue mission."
Key point:
- Autonomous technology must be evaluated together with its human and social consequences.
- The need for human intervention creates responsibilities that cannot be hidden behind the word “game”.
2. Q: How does the chapter distinguish an ordinary racing game from an operationally meaningful simulation?
A: Show answer
Chamal’s VR Café games are primarily entertainment and competition: he learns courses, shifts gears, brakes, drifts, and tries to improve his score. ReelX’s environment looks similar but has a different purpose. Its simulated streets are generated from real-world data, its scenarios include traffic hazards and disasters, and its results are used to assess and train drivers for rescue work. The equipment also reproduces movement and physiological sensations. The distinction is therefore one of purpose, realism, consequences, and the relationship between simulated performance and real operations. A simulation can resemble a game while functioning as a training, assessment, or operational-support system. Calling it a game does not remove the need for safety controls or informed participation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the VR Café and ReelX environments by purpose, data, feedback, risk, evaluation, and connection to real-world activity."
Key point:
- Similar interfaces can conceal very different purposes and consequences.
- The label “game” does not determine whether an activity is low-risk entertainment.
3. Q: Why does ReelX recruit skilled video-game drivers such as Chamal?
A: Show answer
ReelX appears to value rapid visual processing, fine motor control, spatial awareness, route learning, and the ability to make quick adjustments under changing conditions. Chamal has developed these capabilities through repeated VR racing and is able to navigate complex courses efficiently. The company treats those skills as promising inputs for training people to operate autonomous vehicles in exceptional circumstances. The story suggests that game experience can reveal abilities relevant to simulation-based tasks, but it does not establish that a high gaming score proves competence in real driving or emergency rescue. Recruitment should therefore use gaming performance as one screening signal, followed by realistic assessment, safety training, and evaluation of judgment, communication, and emotional readiness.
Study instruction:
- "List the capabilities Chamal demonstrates in racing games and identify which would still require separate validation for rescue driving."
Key point:
- Game performance can reveal potentially transferable skills.
- A gaming record is a screening indicator, not proof of operational competence.
4. Q: Which skills transfer from virtual racing to autonomous-vehicle rescue, and which do not transfer automatically?
A: Show answer
Potentially transferable skills include visual scanning, spatial orientation, rapid response selection, route familiarity, hand-eye coordination, and learning from repeated feedback. Chamal also demonstrates the ability to adapt when obstacles and traffic patterns change. However, a game may not reproduce the full consequences of a real emergency, the ethical weight of carrying passengers, the unpredictability of human behavior, or the emotional effects of injury and loss. It may also reward speed and points when real operations require caution, communication, and prioritization of life safety. Transfer must therefore be tested task by task. A responsible programme should assess not only whether a trainee can perform a maneuver, but whether the trainee knows when not to perform it.
Study instruction:
- "Create a transfer matrix separating game-derived abilities from real-world competencies that need independent training and assessment."
Key point:
- Transfer is partial and depends on the similarity of tasks and conditions.
- Operational judgment and responsibility cannot be inferred from a game score alone.
5. Q: How does adaptive difficulty help Chamal learn, and what safeguards should accompany it?
A: Show answer
Alice explains that the first drive uses a simplified environment and that difficulty increases as Chamal improves. Traffic becomes denser, pedestrians and children appear, and traffic signals behave unpredictably. This progression can help a learner move from basic control to more complex hazard recognition without being overwhelmed immediately. However, increasing difficulty should be governed by measured competence and safe limits, not by a desire to make the experience exciting or extract more performance. The system should define learning objectives, difficulty thresholds, recovery periods, and conditions for pausing. It should also distinguish a trainee’s ability to complete a scenario from the quality and safety of the decisions made along the way.
Study instruction:
- "Design a staged training progression with entry criteria, challenge levels, performance measures, and stop conditions."
Key point:
- Adaptive difficulty can scaffold learning from simple to complex situations.
- Progression must be evidence-based and bounded by safety controls.
6. Q: What is simulation fidelity, and why does it matter in the ReelX training centre?
A: Show answer
Simulation fidelity is the degree to which a training environment reproduces the features of the task that matter for learning or performance. ReelX reproduces a familiar Geely vehicle, local streets, cockpit controls, traffic, pedestrians, and physical feedback. The system is not an exact copy of reality: Alice says the landscape is generated from real data but differs from the road Chamal knows. Fidelity should therefore be judged by the skills being trained, not by visual realism alone. A visually convincing scene may still omit important hazards, social interactions, or system failures. Conversely, a simpler simulation can be useful if it accurately trains a specific decision. Designers should document what the simulation represents, what it omits, and where results may not transfer.
Study instruction:
- "Identify five features of the ReelX simulation and explain which learning objective each feature supports."
Key point:
- Useful fidelity means reproducing task-relevant conditions, not merely creating impressive graphics.
- Every simulation has limits that should be disclosed and tested.
7. Q: How do haptic suits and force-feedback cockpits change the training experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a skintight haptic suit, a helmet, a cockpit that tilts and vibrates, and controls that respond to Chamal’s actions. These devices add bodily sensations to visual and auditory information, making the virtual vehicle feel more immediate. The cockpit’s response may help a trainee associate a control input with acceleration, vibration, or loss of stability. The same realism can also increase stress, fatigue, and confusion between virtual and physical events. Equipment should therefore be calibrated to the learning objective and the individual’s tolerance. Training staff need to explain which sensations are simulated, monitor adverse reactions, and provide a reliable way to stop the session.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how visual, auditory, and haptic feedback contribute to learning, then list the risks of excessive sensory intensity."
Key point:
- Multisensory feedback can make a simulation more immersive and informative.
- Realistic sensations require calibration, monitoring, and a clear stop mechanism.
8. Q: What does synesthesia mean in the story, and how is it used in virtual rescue driving?
A: Show answer
In the story, synesthesia refers to simulated bodily sensations produced through virtual reality, allowing a driver to experience aspects of a virtual event as if they were physical. Junius explains that a degree of body synesthesia can stimulate cognitive capacity and adrenaline, potentially improving performance in a rescue mission. He also explains that the appropriate level varies by driver and mission. The narrative makes clear that the effect can become harmful: Junius deliberately increased the setting during an earthquake mission and later experienced persistent pain associated with the simulated injury. This is a fictional depiction, not a clinical definition or evidence of a proven performance method. The practical lesson is that immersive feedback can affect both body and mind and must be treated as a safety-critical design variable.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the story’s explanation of synesthesia and distinguish its fictional use from a verified medical or engineering claim."
Key point:
- The narrative presents simulated bodily feedback as both a performance aid and a potential hazard.
- Sensory intensity should never be increased without individualized limits and evidence.
9. Q: Why should physiological measurements be monitored during immersive training?
A: Show answer
ReelX displays continuously changing physiological measurements, and Alice tells Chamal to check that his measurements are within a safe zone. Such monitoring can provide warning signals that a trainee is becoming overly stressed, fatigued, or physically uncomfortable. The story later shows Chamal sweating, shaking, and struggling to recover after an intense mission. Physiological data should support—not replace—human observation and direct communication. A safe programme needs defined thresholds, trained staff who can interpret alerts, a process for stopping or reducing intensity, and post-session checks. Because these measurements are personal data, the programme also needs clear rules for collection, access, retention, and use in employment or performance decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a monitoring protocol that identifies indicators, alert thresholds, responsible staff, stop actions, and data-protection rules."
Key point:
- Physiological monitoring can help detect stress and unsafe workload.
- Monitoring is useful only when linked to competent intervention and privacy safeguards.
10. Q: How can a flow state improve performance while also creating danger?
A: Show answer
Chamal enters a state of intense immersion in which the cockpit, vehicle, and virtual environment seem to operate as one system. He feels that the car responds directly to his mind and becomes increasingly absorbed in the task. This concentration helps him perform quickly, but it also narrows his awareness of fatigue and risk. He notices the speedometer only after the vehicle reaches the red zone, then brakes abruptly and experiences a violent crash simulation. The episode shows that strong engagement is not the same as safe performance. Training should teach learners to monitor external indicators, maintain awareness of limits, and interrupt immersion when warning signs appear. Flow can support skill execution, but it must remain subordinate to safety.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the early warning signs that Chamal’s immersion is reducing his situational awareness and propose an interruption routine."
Key point:
- Deep concentration can support performance but may narrow attention.
- Safety checks must remain active even when a task feels effortless.
11. Q: What role does cognitive workload play in Chamal’s first test drive?
A: Show answer
As the simulation becomes more complex, Chamal must monitor traffic, pedestrians, children, signals, speed, and vehicle control. His attention becomes strained and he stops blinking normally, while sweat and sticky palms indicate growing stress. These details show that the task demands more than fast reactions: it requires prioritizing information, detecting hazards, and controlling the vehicle while maintaining awareness of changing conditions. If the environment presents too many signals at once, the trainee may miss critical information or rely on automatic responses. Training designers should manage the number and complexity of simultaneous demands, then increase them progressively as competence develops.
Study instruction:
- "Map the information Chamal must monitor and identify which signals are safety-critical, secondary, or potentially distracting."
Key point:
- Complex driving simulations create competing demands on attention.
- Training should develop prioritization as well as response speed.
12. Q: Why does Chamal’s physical and emotional recovery matter after the tsunami simulation?
A: Show answer
After the tsunami mission, Chamal is drenched in sweat, too exhausted to leave the cockpit without assistance, and later struggles with ordinary activities because his hands shake. The simulated disaster continues to appear in his dreams. These effects show that a virtual task can produce real fatigue and distress even when the events themselves are simulated. A responsible programme must treat recovery as part of the training cycle rather than as unused time. Staff should assess whether the participant has returned to baseline, provide appropriate support, document adverse reactions, and avoid immediate exposure to another intense scenario. A learner’s willingness to continue should not be mistaken for evidence that continued exposure is safe.
Study instruction:
- "Develop post-session recovery checks covering physical symptoms, emotional distress, delayed effects, and clearance to resume training."
Key point:
- Virtual experiences can have real physical and psychological consequences.
- Recovery, debriefing, and fitness-to-resume checks are part of safe training.
13. Q: How can performance incentives such as points and pay distort safety decisions?
A: Show answer
Chamal earns points for rescuing vehicles and knows that higher scores mean more money for his family. During the tsunami simulation, he continues trying to save as many cars as possible even as the wave approaches, and he regrets every vehicle he fails to reach. The incentive makes speed and quantity highly salient, potentially crowding out caution, rest, and realistic prioritization. In an operational programme, rewards should not encourage unsafe risk-taking or penalize a driver for refusing an unreasonable task. Measures should recognize safe decisions, communication, appropriate escalation, and adherence to limits—not just the number of successful interventions. Incentive design is therefore a safety and governance issue, not merely a compensation detail.
Study instruction:
- "Review a performance bonus scheme and test whether it rewards safe judgment or encourages excessive speed and risk exposure."
Key point:
- Poorly designed incentives can push people toward unsafe behavior.
- Performance measures should reward safety, judgment, and appropriate refusal as well as output.
14. Q: Why is a human-in-the-loop arrangement used when autonomous vehicles encounter exceptional conditions?
A: Show answer
The story presents human drivers as a fallback when automated systems face damaged roads, disrupted digital maps, natural disasters, or other unusual events. The human operator can interpret context, make a judgment that is not fully represented in the system’s normal route plan, and take direct control when the scenario demands it. This does not mean that a human is automatically safer or more capable than AI. Human operators can become fatigued, frightened, injured, or misled. A human-in-the-loop design therefore needs a clearly defined role, reliable information, sufficient authority, training, and a safe handover mechanism. It must also specify when the automated system should continue, request assistance, or enter a minimum-risk state.
Study instruction:
- "Define the decision rights and handover conditions for a human operator supporting an autonomous vehicle in an emergency."
Key point:
- Human intervention can provide flexibility in conditions the automated system handles poorly.
- Human fallback requires defined authority, training, and safe handover procedures.
15. Q: What limitations of autonomous systems are illustrated by damaged roads and sudden disasters?
A: Show answer
The story repeatedly places autonomous systems in environments where their assumptions may no longer hold: landslides block roads, an earthquake disrupts navigation, a tsunami disables a smart transport network, and an attack creates smoke and uncertainty near a temple. These events illustrate the difference between routine operation and exceptional conditions. A system that performs well on mapped roads may struggle when physical infrastructure changes, communications fail, or hazards are not represented in its data. The narrative does not prove that current autonomous systems fail in precisely these ways; it uses fictional events to explore a general engineering problem. Resilient design should include degraded modes, fallback plans, emergency communications, and explicit limits on operation.
Study instruction:
- "List the assumptions a route-planning system may rely on and identify what should happen when each assumption becomes false."
Key point:
- Performance in normal conditions does not establish resilience to rare disruptions.
- Exceptional-event planning must include degraded operation and fallback options.
16. Q: How does the story explain the relationship between sensors, control systems, and vehicle movement?
A: Show answer
Zeng Xinlan compares a human body with an autonomous vehicle: sensors, cameras, and LiDAR function like eyes; the control system functions like a brain; and the engine and gears act like legs. The analogy helps Chamal understand how the vehicle observes its surroundings, processes information, and produces movement. It is a simplified explanation rather than a complete technical architecture. Real systems may include additional components, redundant sensors, mapping, localization, planning, diagnostics, and safety controllers. The useful principle is that perception, decision-making, and actuation must work together. A failure in any layer can affect the whole driving task, so safety engineering must test the chain rather than evaluate each component in isolation.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a perception-to-action diagram for an autonomous vehicle and annotate where faults could interrupt the sequence."
Key point:
- Autonomous driving depends on coordinated sensing, computation, and vehicle control.
- System safety requires testing interactions and failure paths across components.
17. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize LiDAR and other sensors in the temple rescue scenario?
A: Show answer
When Yang Juan describes the attack, Junius immediately asks whether smoke or another explosion could reduce LiDAR efficiency and whether terrorists might be concealed among the people needing rescue. His questions show that sensors provide information only within their operating limits. Smoke, obstruction, unusual objects, poor visibility, or damaged infrastructure may make the environment harder to interpret. The story does not provide a technical performance specification for LiDAR, so the point should be treated as a scenario-based risk, not a universal claim about a particular sensor. Safety planning should identify sensor limitations, monitor sensor health, use appropriate redundancy, and define what the vehicle must do when perception becomes uncertain.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sensor-risk register for smoke, obstruction, poor visibility, damaged infrastructure, and inconsistent sensor readings."
Key point:
- Sensor capability depends on environmental conditions and system design.
- Uncertain perception should trigger cautious behavior, not unjustified confidence.
18. Q: What is the significance of smart roads and vehicle-to-infrastructure communication in Shenzhen?
A: Show answer
In the story, Shenzhen’s autonomous vehicles communicate with sensors embedded in roads and with traffic-management systems in the cloud. This connected infrastructure helps coordinate routes, vehicle movement, and emergency responses. Chamal sees that the city’s transport system is not simply a collection of self-driving cars; it is an integrated network involving vehicles, roads, sensors, communications, and centralized coordination. Such an arrangement can support more orderly movement, but it also creates dependencies on power, connectivity, data quality, cybersecurity, and infrastructure maintenance. The chapter presents a speculative future city rather than evidence that every described capability exists in current deployments.
Study instruction:
- "Map the vehicle, road, cloud, and traffic-management components in the story and identify their dependencies and failure points."
Key point:
- Connected mobility depends on infrastructure as well as vehicle intelligence.
- Greater coordination creates benefits and additional system-wide dependencies.
19. Q: How does the ambulance corridor demonstrate coordinated traffic management?
A: Show answer
Chamal observes vehicles moving aside to create a clear path for an ambulance, then returning to their previous positions after it passes. The event illustrates coordinated behavior across many vehicles rather than isolated reactions by one driver. In the story, the response is enabled by communication between vehicles and the surrounding traffic system. The benefit is that emergency access can be created quickly without relying only on each human driver noticing and reacting independently. However, such coordination depends on reliable detection of the emergency vehicle, trustworthy messages, safe spacing, and predictable behavior by all participating vehicles. A real deployment would also need safeguards for conflicting emergency instructions and vehicles that cannot or do not comply.
Study instruction:
- "Specify the information flow and safety checks needed to create and close an emergency vehicle corridor."
Key point:
- Networked coordination can support rapid emergency access.

20. Q: What does the marathon rerouting scene reveal about city-scale autonomous mobility?
A: Show answer
When the city receives a marathon alert, vehicles are rerouted together and traffic reorganizes toward different exits. The scene portrays mobility as a coordinated urban service rather than a set of independent journeys. A city-scale system could use shared information to reduce congestion and manage planned disruptions, but it would also need to account for accessibility, emergency routes, public transport, local restrictions, and people who cannot use the standard service. The narrative emphasizes efficiency and smooth movement; a governance perspective adds questions about who sets priorities, who can override the plan, how errors are corrected, and how the system treats people who are not well represented in its data.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the marathon rerouting scenario from the perspectives of efficiency, accessibility, emergency access, and public accountability."
Key point:
- Urban mobility systems coordinate many interdependent journeys and priorities.
- Efficiency must be balanced with accessibility, resilience, and public oversight.
21. Q: What does Level 5 autonomous driving mean within the chapter’s imagined future?
A: Show answer
Zeng Xinlan describes Shenzhen as a place where most roads and vehicles support L5-level autonomous driving, and she explains that the driver’s seat is no longer essential to every journey. Within the story, this represents a highly automated environment in which vehicles can operate without a human driver performing the driving task. The chapter uses this future setting to explore how vehicle interiors, roads, jobs, and city services may change. Because the source is speculative fiction, its description should not be treated as a current technical certification or a statement that all roads in Shenzhen operate this way today. A careful reader should distinguish the story’s world-building from verified real-world deployment claims.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the chapter uses L5 autonomy as a future-setting device and separate that depiction from claims about present-day capability."
Key point:
