- ILO Social Protection Floors Recommendation
- AI 2041 — publisher description
40. Q: What does current research suggest about AI exposure and the future of jobs?
A: Show answer
The chapter imagines severe automation by 2041, but current labour research distinguishes exposure to AI from the disappearance of an entire occupation. The ILO’s 2025 global index estimates that a substantial share of jobs may be exposed to generative AI, while concluding that job transformation is more likely than full replacement for many occupations. Tasks within a job can be automated, reorganised, or augmented while human responsibility remains. The result will differ across sectors, countries, and skill groups. Policy should therefore monitor task changes and job quality, not rely on a single headline estimate of jobs “lost.”
Study instruction:
- "Choose an occupation and break it into tasks. Mark each task as automatable, augmentable, or strongly dependent on human context, and explain why exposure does not equal job loss."
Key point:
- AI exposure measures potential task change, not a guaranteed number of eliminated jobs.
- The distribution of gains and risks varies by occupation and worker group.
Source links:
- ILO Generative AI and Jobs: A 2025 Update
- OECD AI and the Changing Demand for Skills
41. Q: Why is reskilling not enough on its own to make the transition to plenitude fair?
A: Show answer
Training can help people move into new tasks, but it cannot by itself create enough good jobs, remove discrimination, provide income security, or compensate people for lost bargaining power. Workers may lack time, money, transport, childcare, accessible training, or confidence that a new qualification will lead to work. The OECD finds that AI changes skill demand even in occupations that do not require specialist AI expertise. Effective transition policy therefore combines training with job matching, income support, worker voice, recognition of prior learning, regional investment, and protection against unfair treatment.
Study instruction:
- "Design a reskilling pathway for a worker whose routine tasks are being automated. Include funding, time away from work, accessible delivery, recognised credentials, and a route into an actual vacancy."
Key point:
- Training is necessary but must connect to jobs and wider social support.
- Transition policy should address access barriers and the quality of new work.
Source links:
- OECD AI and the Changing Demand for Skills
- ILO Generative AI and Jobs: A 2025 Update
42. Q: Why might care and relationship-based work remain important in a highly automated economy?
A: Show answer
Care work involves attention to another person’s needs, trust, emotional understanding, physical assistance, and context-sensitive judgment. AI can help schedule visits, monitor safety, translate information, or reduce administrative work, but it cannot automatically provide the human relationship that makes care respectful and meaningful. The chapter places Keira’s companionship at the centre of a future where many routine jobs have been automated. This does not mean care jobs are guaranteed to be secure or well paid. Policy must value them through adequate pay, training, staffing, worker protections, and support for informal carers.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which parts of a care worker’s day could be supported by technology and which require human presence or judgment. Then propose one policy that improves the quality of care work."
Key point:
- Automation can support care but should not erase its relational core.
- Care work needs fair pay, training, and protections to remain sustainable.
Source links:
- WHO Dementia Fact Sheet
- ILO Generative AI and Jobs: A 2025 Update
43. Q: How can a society measure whether plenitude is improving people’s lives?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the idea that income or consumption alone captures a good life. The OECD’s 2025 guidelines distinguish three components of subjective well-being: life evaluation, affect, and eudaimonia. Life evaluation concerns how people assess their lives; affect concerns experienced feelings; and eudaimonia concerns meaning, purpose, autonomy, and functioning well. These measures complement objective indicators such as health, education, housing, safety, environmental quality, and social connection. A policy should use a balanced dashboard rather than one number that can conceal hardship or cultural differences.
Study instruction:
- "Build a small well-being dashboard with one indicator for life evaluation, one for affect, one for meaning, and three objective living-condition indicators. Explain what each measure misses."
Key point:
- Well-being is multidimensional and cannot be represented by income or one score alone.
- Subjective measures complement, rather than replace, objective social indicators.
Source links:
- OECD Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being
- OECD 2025 Subjective Well-being Guidelines — Overview
44. Q: Why should a well-being programme report distributions and group differences rather than only an average score?
A: Show answer
An average can rise even while the most disadvantaged group falls further behind. The OECD guidelines recommend looking beyond central tendency to the distribution of subjective well-being and to differences across population groups. For a programme like Jukurrpa, administrators should examine outcomes by age, gender, disability, ethnicity, location, and other relevant factors, while checking sample sizes and statistical uncertainty. Community consultation and qualitative evidence can explain why a score differs. A programme is not equitable merely because its national average improves.
Study instruction:
- "Imagine the average Moola score increases after a policy change. List three subgroup comparisons and two qualitative questions you would examine before declaring the programme successful."
Key point:
- Averages can conceal unequal gains and unequal burdens.
- Distributional analysis and community evidence are essential for judging fairness.
Source links:
- OECD Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
45. Q: What privacy risks arise when a smart home and wristband collect continuous behavioural and health data?
A: Show answer
Continuous sensors can reveal routines, mobility, sleep, medication habits, cognitive changes, social contacts, and emotional states. Even if each data point seems harmless, combined records can expose intimate details or be repurposed for employment, insurance, policing, or commercial profiling. The story’s wristband and home system therefore create a power imbalance between the person being monitored and the institutions that interpret the data. Responsible design requires data minimisation, a clear purpose, informed consent, security, limited retention, access controls, and a way to refuse or withdraw without losing essential care.
Study instruction:
- "Create a data inventory for Joanna’s smart home. For each data type, state why it is needed, who can access it, how long it is retained, and what harm could follow from misuse."
Key point:
- Health and behavioural data can reveal far more than a single measurement suggests.
- Consent and privacy protections must remain meaningful throughout the data life cycle.
Source links:
- WHO Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
46. Q: What does the WHO guidance require when AI is used in health and care?
A: Show answer
WHO’s guidance recognises potential benefits in diagnosis, treatment, research, and public health, but insists that ethics and human rights shape design and deployment. Its principles include protecting human autonomy, promoting well-being and safety, ensuring transparency, assigning responsibility, supporting inclusiveness and equity, and making systems responsive and sustainable. In the story, smart-home alerts and genomic precision treatment are fictional capabilities; real clinical use would need evidence for a defined purpose, regulatory review, trained professionals, and monitoring after deployment. AI should support healthcare workers and patients, not replace accountability for medical decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Review the WHO principles and map each one to a possible risk in Joanna’s smart-home or precision-care scenario. Identify which principle would most directly prevent each risk."
Key point:
- Health AI must be safe, evidence-based, inclusive, and accountable.
- Human autonomy and professional responsibility remain central to care.
Source links:
- WHO Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO Dementia Fact Sheet
47. Q: What are federated learning and trusted execution environments, and why does the chapter mention them?
A: Show answer
The chapter imagines Moola processing sensitive information on a wristband using privacy-preserving computing. Federated learning generally allows models to be trained across distributed devices or institutions without routinely centralising all raw data, although model updates can still leak information if not protected. A trusted execution environment is a hardware-supported area designed to isolate code and data during processing. Neither method is a complete privacy guarantee: implementation flaws, compromised devices, insecure outputs, poor access controls, or weak governance can still expose information. Privacy claims should be tested against a clear threat model.
Study instruction:
- "Explain federated learning and a trusted execution environment in plain language. Then list one threat each can reduce and one threat that still requires separate controls."
Key point:
- Privacy-preserving computation can reduce some data exposure but is not risk-free.
- Technical controls must be paired with governance, testing, and accountability.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
48. Q: How should a person’s consent be handled when a public programme makes digital participation necessary?
A: Show answer
Consent is not meaningful if refusing data collection means losing access to food, housing, healthcare, or other essential support. A programme should explain what is collected, why it is needed, how decisions are made, who receives the information, and what alternatives exist. It should offer non-digital access where feasible, minimise mandatory data, and separate eligibility for basic rights from optional participation in a scoring system. The chapter’s wristband illustrates how a helpful service can become a surveillance tool when the same device records speech and assigns social value.
Study instruction:
- "Test a proposed consent screen by asking whether a participant can refuse optional data uses without losing essential support. Rewrite any statement that is vague or bundles unrelated permissions."
Key point:
- Power imbalance can make nominal consent coercive.
- Essential services should not be conditioned on unnecessary personal-data collection.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- WHO Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
49. Q: Why might an AI system that tries to maximise happiness produce the wrong outcome?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s Isle of Happiness discussion warns that an algorithm can satisfy immediate preferences while undermining longer-term meaning, challenge, relationships, or autonomy. People adapt to repeated rewards, so more stimulation may produce diminishing satisfaction rather than lasting well-being. A system that optimises a narrow metric can also encourage dependency, manipulation, or avoidance of difficult but worthwhile experiences. The chapter therefore contrasts pleasure with purpose and self-development. A responsible design should ask whose definition of happiness is being optimised, what the system cannot measure, and whether users can reject its recommendations.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one happiness metric, such as daily mood or engagement time. Explain how optimising it could harm autonomy or long-term well-being, and propose two safeguards."
Key point:
- Short-term preference satisfaction is not the same as lasting well-being.
- AI should support human choice rather than define happiness for the user.
Source links:
- OECD Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
50. Q: What does the chapter mean by hedonic adaptation, and how does it challenge a simple abundance model?
A: Show answer
Hedonic adaptation describes how people can become accustomed to changes in circumstances, so a benefit that initially feels extraordinary may later become normal. The chapter uses the idea to question whether giving people ever more luxury, entertainment, or sensory stimulation will create lasting happiness. If rewards escalate continuously, people may reset their expectations and experience less satisfaction from ordinary life. This does not mean material improvements are unimportant: security and relief from deprivation matter greatly. It means that abundance should be paired with relationships, meaningful activity, autonomy, and opportunities to grow.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one improvement that could raise well-being immediately and one factor that may influence how long the effect lasts. Distinguish adaptation from the claim that material security has no value."
Key point:
- Adaptation can reduce the lasting effect of repeated rewards.
- Security matters, but flourishing also depends on meaning, connection, and agency.
Source links:
- OECD Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being
- AI 2041 — publisher description
51. Q: How should corporations respond if technology makes some goods dramatically cheaper to produce?
A: Show answer
A firm could pass savings to customers, improve quality, invest in new products, reduce environmental impacts, or preserve high prices through market power and product differentiation. The chapter warns that companies may create artificial scarcity through controlled supply, exclusive access, or marketing that makes ordinary goods seem obsolete. These are risks to examine, not proof that every high price is abusive. Competition authorities assess market power, barriers to entry, exclusionary conduct, and consumer effects. In a plenitude transition, policy should encourage innovation while preventing durable market power from blocking broad access to cost reductions.
Study instruction:
- "Take a product with falling production costs. Compare a competitive outcome with a market dominated by one supplier, and identify the evidence a regulator would need before alleging anticompetitive conduct."
Key point:
- Falling production cost does not guarantee falling consumer prices.
- Competition policy helps ensure cost reductions translate into choice, quality, and consumer benefit.
Source links:
- OECD Competition Enforcement
- AI 2041 — publisher description
52. Q: What is artificial scarcity, and how can it arise even when production becomes inexpensive?
A: Show answer
Artificial scarcity occurs when supply or access is deliberately restricted despite the ability to produce more, often to preserve price, exclusivity, or status. The chapter points to controlled release of goods, product tiers, fashion cycles, and destruction of unsold stock as examples of how businesses may protect margins or stimulate demand. Some differentiation can reflect genuine quality, innovation, or cost differences, so not every premium price is artificial scarcity. The important questions are whether consumers have realistic alternatives, whether entry is blocked, and whether restrictions harm competition or welfare.
Study instruction:
- "Select one market and distinguish natural scarcity, quality differentiation, and potentially artificial restriction. Identify what facts would be needed before drawing a conclusion."
Key point:
- Scarcity can be physical, institutional, or deliberately maintained.
- Market analysis must distinguish legitimate differentiation from exclusionary conduct.
Source links:
- OECD Competition Enforcement
- UNEP Global Resources Outlook 2024
53. Q: Why could a transition toward very low prices create problems for financial institutions?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that a rapid collapse in prices could disrupt institutions built around wages, debt, savings, taxes, asset values, and corporate revenues. Deflation can increase the real burden of fixed nominal debts and weaken spending if households and firms postpone purchases while expecting prices to fall further. However, lower prices caused by productivity gains are not automatically harmful; the effect depends on incomes, debt structures, monetary policy, and how the transition is managed. The point is that abundance requires financial reform and careful sequencing, not an uncontrolled collapse of existing contracts and institutions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how falling prices can help consumers but hurt a borrower with fixed nominal debt. Identify two policies or institutional changes that could reduce transition risk."
Key point:
- Productivity-driven cost declines and economy-wide deflation are not the same thing.
- Financial stability depends on debt, income, expectations, and policy responses.
Source links:
- BIS — Next-generation Monetary and Financial System
- ILO Social Protection Floors Recommendation
54. Q: How might digital money and tokenisation change financial infrastructure without eliminating the need for trust?
A: Show answer
Tokenisation represents assets or claims in digital form on programmable platforms, potentially integrating messaging, reconciliation, and settlement. The BIS describes a possible system combining tokenised central-bank reserves, commercial-bank money, and government bonds while preserving trust in central-bank money. Such infrastructure may improve some payments and settlement processes, but it does not automatically create a fair distribution system or remove financial risk. Users still need clear legal rights, reliable redemption, operational resilience, privacy safeguards, and oversight against fraud and illicit finance. Digital form changes the rails; it does not remove the need for trusted institutions.
Study instruction:
- "Define tokenisation in one sentence. Then identify which parts of a payment or securities transaction could be integrated and which legal, operational, and trust questions remain."
Key point:
- Tokenisation can change how financial claims are recorded and settled.
- Digital money still depends on legal certainty, trust, integrity, and sound governance.
Source links:
- BIS — Tokenised Unified Ledger
- BIS — Virtual Currencies and the Functions of Money
55. Q: Why does the chapter treat the technological singularity as uncertain rather than inevitable?
A: Show answer
The singularity is a hypothesis that machine intelligence could surpass human intelligence and trigger rapid, self-reinforcing change. The chapter distinguishes this possibility from a settled forecast, arguing that faster computing alone does not guarantee the scientific breakthroughs needed for broadly capable intelligence. Progress in reasoning, creativity, strategic judgment, empathy, trust, and consciousness raises questions that cannot be answered by simply adding more processors. The exact timeline and nature of any such transition remain contested. Responsible analysis separates demonstrated capabilities from speculative projections and prepares for risk without treating one future as predetermined.
Study instruction:
- "Write two columns: current demonstrated AI capabilities and hypothetical capabilities associated with a singularity. Place each claim in the correct column and state what evidence would move it."
Key point:
- The singularity is a contested scenario, not a confirmed date or outcome.
- Computing power alone does not settle questions about general intelligence or consciousness.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
56. Q: What scientific capabilities beyond raw computing power would be needed for more general AI?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights creativity, strategic reasoning, counterfactual thinking, empathy, compassion, trust, consciousness, and the ability to understand human needs and emotions. These are not simply larger versions of pattern recognition; they involve different forms of representation, learning, judgment, and social context. Even where systems perform well on specific benchmarks, that does not establish that they possess human-like understanding or subjective experience. A careful assessment asks what task is being measured, what environment the system was tested in, and whether performance generalises safely. This guards against confusing impressive demonstrations with a complete theory of intelligence.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one capability named in the chapter, such as counterfactual reasoning or empathy. Define how it might be tested and explain why a benchmark score would not prove that the system experiences it as a human does."
Key point:
- General intelligence involves more than scaling a single technical capability.
- Task performance should not be confused with consciousness or human experience.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
57. Q: What does the chapter mean when it says that humans remain the authors of the AI future?
A: Show answer
The closing argument is that AI’s effects depend on the goals, incentives, rules, and social choices that people build around it. Technology can expand capacity, but people and institutions decide whether the gains support security, dignity, justice, and meaningful lives or reinforce exclusion and concentrated power. Human agency means more than individual optimism: it includes public accountability, worker participation, cultural rights, education, democratic oversight, and the ability to challenge harmful systems. The chapter rejects both passive surrender and automatic techno-utopianism. It asks readers to shape institutions so that AI complements rather than displaces human flourishing.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one decision each for governments, businesses, workers, and communities that would influence whether AI expands or reduces human agency."
Key point:
- AI outcomes are shaped by human choices and institutional design.
- Human agency requires rights, accountability, participation, and meaningful alternatives.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
58. Q: What practical roadmap does the chapter imply for an inclusive transition to plenitude?
A: Show answer
A credible roadmap would combine affordable clean energy with reliable grids, responsible materials use, and productive automation. It would guarantee a social-protection floor, support workers through accessible lifelong learning, and recognise care and community contribution without turning them into coercive scores. It would protect privacy, cultural rights, competition, and environmental limits, while measuring both objective living conditions and subjective well-being. The sequence matters: build infrastructure, distribute basic security, invite public participation, test programmes, monitor unequal effects, and revise designs when incentives produce gaming or exclusion. Plenitude should be judged by whether people gain real options, not merely by whether technology becomes cheaper.
Study instruction:
- "Turn the answer into a six-stage policy plan. For each stage, name a lead institution, a measurable outcome, and a safeguard for groups most likely to be left behind."
Key point:
- Technology, social protection, and governance must advance together.
- A fair transition needs measurable outcomes, public participation, and iterative correction.
Source links:
- IEA Batteries and Secure Energy Transitions
- ILO Social Protection Floors Recommendation
59. Q: What final lesson should readers take from the chapter’s contrast between abundance and human purpose?
A: Show answer
The chapter closes by arguing that freedom from routine work and material insecurity should create room for deeper human goals, not a new competition to accumulate status. Keira’s artistic ambitions, Joanna’s conservation work, and their relationship show that people may flourish through learning, care, cultural connection, creativity, and contribution. Yet these outcomes are not automatic: institutions can still reward vanity, exclude marginalised groups, or define success too narrowly. The chapter’s vision is therefore conditional. Abundance becomes meaningful when people retain agency and can pursue purposes they value while respecting the rights and needs of others.
Study instruction:
- "Write a short reflection naming one form of human purpose that could become more accessible in a high-automation society. Then identify one policy or cultural condition needed to make that opportunity genuinely inclusive."
Key point:
- Material abundance is a means, not the final measure of a good society.
- Human flourishing depends on choice, connection, meaning, justice, and shared responsibility.
Source links:
- AI 2041 — publisher description
- OECD Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being
